# Chapter 1

## **Chapter 1**

The priest refused to bury my mother in the dress she was found wearing. It was the one she wore the night she met my father, the silk still holding the ghost of his cigar smoke and the jasmine from her hair. To inter her in it, he said, would be to consecrate the sin. So I stole it back from the mortuary, the fabric cold as a confession against my skin.


I watched her from the veranda, the ghost of her teacup warm in my hands. The air was the same temperature as blood. It was the hour when the light bled out of the sky and the world turned monochrome, all greens and blacks and the bruised purple of distant mountains. The house behind me—La Ceniza, The Ashes—breathed a damp, wooden sigh. It was a colonial skeleton, its whitewash peeling like a sunburn, its bones full of the whispers of termites. It had been in our family since the Conquest, or so the story went. A story, like all our stories, that was probably a lie wrapped in a truth, buried in silt.

“Catalina.” My father’s voice was a dry thing, scraped from the back of his throat. He stood in the doorway, a silhouette against the lamplight. “She’ll catch a fever.”

“The fever is already in her,” I said, not turning. “It’s just a different kind.”

He didn’t argue. He never argued with me about her. It was our one treaty. He came to stand beside me, the scent of his pipe tobacco—a bitter, local leaf—mixing with the perfume of night-blooming jasmine. He was a historian, a collector of dead things. Our house was a museum of fragments: pre-Columbian pottery shards, colonial-era letters faded to ghosts, bones of unidentifiable small animals. He believed the past was a puzzle that could be solved if you had all the pieces. I believed the past was the river: it moved, it changed course, it drowned you, and it never gave back what it took.

“Tomás is dead,” my father stated. “Or his boat is. No one has seen the *María Sangrante* in a week.”

“Then she’ll wait until she becomes a tree,” I said. “And we can hang a plaque on her: ‘Here stood Soledad, who waited for what never arrives.’”

A soft, wet sound escaped him. It might have been a laugh. “You have her cruelty.”

“I have her eyes,” I corrected. “The cruelty I cultivated on my own.”

On the bank, my mother raised a hand. Not to us. To the emptiness over the water. A greeting, or a summons. The jungle across the way exhaled a cloud of fireflies, a sudden constellation low to the water, and for a moment, her slender form was etched in specks of cold gold. She looked like a saint in a crumbling fresco, moments from fading into the wall.

Then, a sound. Not from the river, but from the road—the single, muddy track that connected La Ceniza to the town of Puerto Viejo, twenty kilometers away. The groan of an engine pushed past its limits, the suck of tires in deep muck. Headlights cut through the twilight, swinging wildly like searchlights.

My mother turned, her attention broken. My father stiffened. Visitors were rare, and never good. The last had been the tax assessor, who’d taken one look at the sagging roof and the haunted air and halved the property value on the spot.

The vehicle that lurched into the clearing was a Jeep from another era, its canvas roof torn, its body a topography of rust and dents. It coughed, shuddered, and died. The door opened.

A man unfolded himself from the driver’s seat. He was tall, too tall for the low door, and had to duck. He wore a linen suit the color of cream, now spattered with mud up to the knees. He carried no bag, only a leather folio clutched under one arm. He looked at our house, at the weeping façade, at the wild orchids strangling the columns, and then at my mother, standing barefoot in the river mud. He removed his hat—a Panama hat, gone limp with humidity.

“I am looking for Professor Emilio Vergara,” he called out. His Spanish was precise, Castilian, but with an accent I couldn’t place. Something musical hiding in the consonants.

“You have found his house,” my father answered, his voice reclaiming its academic authority. “I am he.”

The man picked his way across the yard, avoiding the worst of the puddles with an unerring grace that seemed absurd given the setting. “My name is León Sombra,” he said, stopping at the foot of the veranda steps. His eyes, even in the gloom, were a startling, pale grey. Like quartz, or ice over a deep pool. They swept over me, a brief, assessing glance, before returning to my father. “I have come about the library.”

A silence fell, thicker than the humidity. The library was not spoken of. It was the heart of La Ceniza, and like a real heart, it was a dark, private chamber. My father’s life’s work, and his mania. A collection of books and manuscripts so rare, so peculiar, and in some cases, so infamous, that their existence was a carefully guarded rumor.

“There is no library here,” my father said, too quickly.

León Sombra smiled. It was a small, sorrowful thing. “Professor, please. The *Biblioteca de los Susurros*. The Library of Whispers. I have traveled a very long way. From Cádiz, to Caracas, to here. I did not come for the scenery.”

“Who sent you?”

“A mutual friend. Father Alonso, of the Archivo de Indias. He said if any man alive could help me, it would be you. He said you possessed the… *espejo oscuro*. The dark mirror.”

My father went very still. I felt the shift in him, a tectonic quiet. My mother, forgotten on the bank, began to walk slowly toward the house, her feet leaving perfect, vulnerable prints in the mud.

“Help you with what?” I asked. My voice cut the tension like a knife.

León’s quartz eyes found me again. This time, they lingered. “A translation,” he said. “Of a text that should not exist.”

My father let out a long, slow breath. “Come inside,” he said. “The mosquitoes are feasting on your city blood.”

In the sala, the single oil lamp painted us in Rembrandt shadows. My mother had vanished, presumably to her room. The house seemed to listen. León Sombra sat in my father’s best chair, the one with the horsehair stuffing leaking from a seam, and accepted a glass of aguardiente without comment. He drank it like water.

“The text,” my father prompted, sitting opposite him. I leaned against the doorframe, a spectator.

León placed his leather folio on the small table between them. He opened it with reverent care. Inside was not a book, but a sheaf of loose, thick pages. They were not paper. They were skin. I knew it instantly. Pale, poreless, covered in a script that was both fluid and angular, a language of loops and slashes that looked like it had been written with a scalpel.

“It is called *El Libro de los Hilos Rotos*,” León said softly. “The Book of Broken Threads. It is a heretical text, supposedly burned in Quito in 1623. It purports to be an account, not of lives, but of the moments where lives… unravel. Where fate is cut. Not death, but the instant of mortal decision that *leads* to death. Or to damnation.”

“A book of mortality,” my father whispered, his scholar’s hunger igniting in his eyes. He did not reach for it. He was afraid.

“More than that,” León said. “It is said to be indexed. Not by name, or date. But by… resonance. By the sound a soul makes when its thread is snipped. I need to find a specific entry. From this region. From 1789.”

“Why?” The question was out of me before I could stop it.

León looked at me. His gaze was unnervingly direct. “Because I believe it contains the final moments of my many-times-great-grandfather, a Spanish magistrate who vanished in this province. His name was Don Álvaro Sombra. He did not simply die. He was… *unmade*. His family line has been plagued by a singular affliction ever since. We die, Miss Vergara, but we do not *end*. We linger. A family of ghosts haunting ourselves. I wish to know what he did, what decision he made, to curse us so. I wish to find the thread he broke, and if possible, tie it back.”

He said it with the calm of a man discussing a faulty engine. My father was enthralled. I was repulsed. It was the kind of story that clung to this land, a parasitic vine on the tree of history.

“The dark mirror is not a translation tool,” my father said, his voice low. “It is a lens. A way of reading what is written *beneath* the text. The stains, the impressions, the whispers in the margins. It is fragile. And dangerous.”

“I am familiar with danger, Professor.”

“Are you?” I pushed off the doorframe. “The things in my father’s library… they aren’t just old. They’re *hungry*. They’ve been waiting in the dark, and a man with a family ghost story looks an awful lot like supper.”

A genuine smile touched León’s lips, and for a moment, he looked his age—perhaps mid-thirties—and less like an emissary from a graveyard. “Then it is fortunate I have no appetite left,” he said. “And that I am already, in some ways, a ghost. Will you help me?”

He was asking my father, but his eyes were on me. Challenging me. Daring me to believe him.

From the depths of the house, my mother began to sing. A lullaby, off-key and in a language that might have been Portuguese, or might have been something older. It was the sound of a mind unspooling.

My father looked from the skin-pages to León’s pale, determined face. He looked at me, his daughter, the guardian of this crumbling asylum. He looked toward the sound of his wife’s voice, a thread forever fraying.

“We begin at dawn,” my father said, his voice heavy with inevitability. “The mirror does not work well in artificial light. It requires the sun. Catalina will assist you. She reads the under-text better than I do. Her eyes are… younger.”

León nodded, a formal little bow of his head. “Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me,” I said, turning to leave the room. “The river gives nothing back. And this house only takes. You’ve just offered it a new thing to chew on. I hope you taste bitter.”

I left them there, two men surrounded by shadows and the smell of rot and aguardiente. I walked out onto the veranda. The night was complete now, a velvet blanket. The fireflies were gone. On the riverbank, where my mother had stood, I saw two small, dark shapes. Her satin slippers. The river, that slow brown god, had spat them back onto the mud.

It was not a kindness. It was a warning. Some things are rejected by the deep.

# Chapter 2

## **Chapter 2**

Dawn at La Ceniza was not a sunrise but a slow, green leakage. Light didn’t break; it seeped through the canopy, diluted by a million leaves, arriving in the house tired and moss-colored. The library, however, existed in perpetual twilight.

It was not a room one entered. One descended into it. A narrow, spiraling stone staircase led down from a trapdoor in my father’s study, into the cool, subterranean belly of the house. The air there was a different creature—cold, dry, smelling of stone, slow decay, and the faint, almond-like scent of arsenic, which my father used to deter insects. The walls were lined not with elegant shelves, but with rough-hewn niches and iron-bound chests. Books were wrapped in oilcloth, manuscripts sealed in tin boxes. It was less a library than a catacomb for words.

León Sombra followed me down, his city shoes whispering on the damp stone. He had said nothing all morning, only observed. He had taken coffee with us in the sala, his eyes cataloguing the water stains on the ceiling, the way my mother stirred her spoon in endless, concentric circles, the dust motes swimming in the weak light. He was a scribe of silence.

My father was already below, hunched over a wide, stone table. On it lay the skin-pages of *El Libro de los Hilos Rotos*, carefully weighted at the corners with smooth river stones. Beside them rested the *espejo oscuro*. It was not a mirror in any conventional sense. It was a slab of obsidian, roughly the size of a folio, polished to a liquid black sheen. Its surface did not reflect; it swallowed light, and in doing so, revealed what light obscured.

“The vellum is human,” my father said without preamble, as if discussing the grain of oak. “Female, I’d guess. Young. The script is a hybrid—a bastardized Latin phoneticism applied to a pre-Columbian symbolic language. Probably Quechua root. The heresy is in the synthesis. The Church burned it for blending the grammar of salvation with the vocabulary of fatalism.”

León approached the table but did not touch. “Can you read it?”

“The surface text? In parts. It is a liturgy of endings. ‘And the thread was cut at the hour of the bull… the woman turned toward the mountain and her voice became stone…’ Poetic, but oblique. The truth, if there is one, will be in the sub-text. The impressions left by the scribe’s hand, the traces of the ink that has faded, the history of the skin itself. Catalina.”

I stepped forward. This was my duty, my curse. My eyes had always seen the ghost in the machine, the watermark on the soul. I could look at a forged letter and see the tremor of the forger’s guilt in the flourish of a ‘G’. I could read a love note and taste the salt of the tears that had fallen on it. My father called it a ‘palimpsest intuition.’ I called it a sickness.

I took my seat before the obsidian slab. My father, with tweezers and the hands of a surgeon, lifted the first skin-page and placed it gently over the dark stone. The blackness beneath seemed to bleed into the pale vellum, deepening its color, making the brown ink glow with a faint, phosphorescent resentment.

“Now,” my father breathed.

I leaned close. I let my focus soften, not reading the words, but *inhaling* the page. The script writhed. Beneath the elegant strokes, I saw other things: the frantic pressure of the quill digging in fear, a thumbprint smudged in something dark—ochre, or blood—at the corner, the ghost of a previous line, erased but not forgotten. Words beneath words.

“He was afraid,” I murmured, my voice sounding strange in the crypt-like air. “The scribe. His hand is steady but… here, and here… the letters cramp. He was writing something he believed would be found. And punished.”

“What does it say?” León’s voice was close to my ear. I could smell him now, over the almond and the dust: a clean, starched linen smell, and beneath it, something cold, like wet earth in a deep cellar.

I traced a line with my gaze, letting the sub-text rise. “The surface says: ‘The magistrate rode under a sun of iron.’ Beneath that, it whispers: ‘He carried a secret in a box of cedar. The secret had a weight, and the weight had a heartbeat.’”

I moved to the next line. The obsidian pulled the hidden story to the surface like a bruise. “Surface: ‘He sought the city of mirrors.’ Sub-text: ‘He sought the place where the world repeats itself, to hide the thing that had no double.’”

León’s breath caught. “Go on.”

We worked for hours. The world above ceased to exist. There was only the slow, grueling séance of the page. My eyes began to ache, a deep, throbbing pain behind them. The story of Don Álvaro Sombra emerged in fragments, a mosaic of dread.

He was not just a magistrate. He was a purifier, sent to root out a sect that worshipped not saints, but shadows—los Adoradores de la Sombra, the very name a dark joke on his own. They believed that at the moment of a person’s mortal decision, a twin possibility was born, a ghost-self that lived the unchosen life. Their ‘worship’ was an attempt to commune with these forsaken twins, these echo-souls.

Don Álvaro had captured their leader, a woman known only as La Tejedora—The Weaver. The surface text stated he executed her for heresy. The sub-text, bleeding black in the obsidian mirror, told another tale.

“He did not kill her,” I read, my throat dry. “He… *contained* her. The cedar box. It was not for a secret. It was for *her*. Or for… the part of her that could weave the threads.” I looked up, the phantom images of the text swimming in my vision. “He stole her power. Or tried to. To use it. The text is unclear, chaotic here. The scribe’s terror is overwhelming. It says… ‘He opened the box at the confluence of rivers, where time is a circle. He sought to cut his own thread of mortality, to send his death away as an echo. But the weave… recoiled.’”

“What does that mean?” León demanded, his composure cracking for the first time. “*Recoiled*?”

My father interjected, his face ashen. “It means he tried to cheat death by forcing his mortal moment onto a ghostly twin. A theological abomination. He tried to become the weaver. But you cannot steal a power rooted in sacrifice without making a sacrifice of your own.”

I looked back at the mirror. The final lines on the page were a storm of sub-text, the hidden story screaming to be heard. I squinted, pushing through the pain in my skull. The words formed, cold and clear.

“It didn’t recoil *away* from him,” I whispered. “It recoiled *through* him. Through his blood. ‘The curse is not upon him, but within the line. The broken thread is not severed; it is frayed. Each successor inherits the unraveling. They live, they die, but their echo… their echo *lingers*. A family of after-images, forever watching the life they should have had.’”

The silence in the library was absolute. The truth hung in the cold air between us: León was not haunted by an ancestor’s ghost. He *was* the ghost. The echo. The unchosen possibility made flesh, doomed to repeat a half-life, trailing the scent of wet earth and endless regret.

León stepped back from the table. He looked at his own hands as if seeing them for the first time. “So there is no tying it back,” he said, his voice flat. “The thread is not broken. It is… unraveling. In perpetuity.”

“There might be a way,” my father said, a desperate, scholarly zeal in his eyes. “If we can find the confluence. The place where he performed the ritual. If we can find the box, or its remains—”

“No.”

The word came from the staircase. My mother stood on the bottom step, barefoot, her hair loose. She was not singing. Her eyes were lucid, sharp as flint. She rarely came down here. The library frightened her.

“Soledad, my dear—” my father began.

She ignored him, her gaze fixed on León. “You bring the unraveling into my house,” she said, her voice clear and cold. “I can smell it on you. Like ozone after lightning. You are not a man with a ghost. You are a ghost who forgot to die. And you will pull at the threads here. You will make everything come loose.” Her eyes shifted to me, filled with a terrible, prescient love. “You will fray *her*.”

Then, as suddenly as it came, the lucidity shattered. Her face went soft, vague. She hummed a snippet of the lullaby and wandered back up the stairs, leaving a new kind of silence behind.

León looked at me, his grey eyes wide with a horror that mirrored my own. My father busied himself with the skin-pages, muttering about confluence points and historical maps.

But I was staring at the obsidian mirror. In its black depth, after my mother’s words, I had seen something else. A fleeting impression on the page we had just read, rising and vanishing like a bubble from a swamp.

It was not about Don Álvaro.

It was a line of sub-text I had missed, written in a panic at the very edge of the vellum. It contained a name, a name that was not 300 years old.

It was mine.

*Catalina*.

And beside it, two words: *la sucesora*. The successor.

# Chapter 3

## **Chapter 3**

The heat of the day was a physical weight, but the cold from the library had entered my bones. I fled to the riverbank, to the one place in La Ceniza that felt like a boundary, an edge. The brown water slid past, indifferent. My mother’s retrieved slippers sat side-by-side on a root, placed with a neatness that felt accusatory. *La sucesora*. The word echoed in the chamber of my skull. A successor to what? To a curse? To a library of whispers? To a madness?

I heard his footsteps on the grass behind me before I saw his shadow fall across the water.

“You saw it too,” León said. It wasn’t a question.

I didn’t turn. “Go away, ghost.”

“I am not a ghost. Not in the way you think.”

“You’re worse. You’re a man who is also a rumor of a man. You’re a walking sub-text.” I finally looked at him. The morning sun highlighted the fine lines at the corners of his eyes, the real, human stubble on his jaw. He was solid. He cast a shadow. And yet, my mother’s words clung to him. *Ozone after lightning*. “What does it mean? My name.”

“I don’t know.” He came to stand beside me, not too close. He watched the river as if expecting it to answer. “The book records moments of unraveling. Decisions that fracture a life-line. Perhaps your life-line is… adjacent to mine, now. Perhaps by seeking my own truth, I have brushed against yours. I am sorry.”

His apology was infuriating in its sincerity. “Sorry? You drag your metaphysical disease into our home, you stir up the shadows in my father’s crypt, you frighten my mother into a moment of horrible clarity, and you find my name scribbled in the invisible ink of a damned book, and you’re *sorry*?”

“Yes,” he said simply. “I am. But I cannot un-know it. And neither can you.”

He was right. The knowledge was a stain. I could feel it, a new tension in the air of La Ceniza, a fresh warp in its already-slanted reality. The house itself felt watchful.

“Your mother is not mad,” he said after a while. “She is a seamstress in a house of unraveling threads. She feels the pulls and snags the rest of us cannot see.”

“You diagnose my family after one day?”

“I recognize the landscape.” He turned to face me fully. “Catalina, I need to find the confluence. The place where it happened. Your father believes he can triangulate it using colonial survey maps and the description in the text. ‘Where the twin rivers embrace the stone lion.’ He is upstairs now, looking.”

“And if you find it? What then? Do you think you’ll find a magic box and say a prayer and your echo will pop like a soap bubble?”

“I think,” he said, his voice dropping, “that I might find a place where the veil is thin. Where I can see what he did. Where I can… speak to the echo. To *him*. To understand the choice he made, so I can make a different one.”

It was the first truly human thing he’d said—a plea for understanding, not a scholarly quest. He was a man trying to read his own obituary before it was written.

Before I could answer, a shout came from the house. My father’s voice, sharp with excitement. “León! Catalina! Come quickly!”

We found him in his study, maps and brittle parchments spread across every surface. His eyes were fever-bright. “It’s not a metaphor,” he said, jabbing a finger at a hand-drawn map from 1745. “The Stone Lion. *El León de Piedra*. It was a landmark. A rock formation at the fork where the Río Sombra meets the Río Espíritu—the Shadow River meets the Spirit River. Here!” His finger landed on a spot roughly fifteen kilometers upstream, deep in the jungle where no roads went. “The confluence. It was noted by the early friars as a ‘place of pagan congregation.’ It must be it.”

León studied the map. “Is it accessible?”

“Tomás’s boat could take us up the Sombra to within a few kilometers. Then we walk.” My father’s enthusiasm was a brittle thing. It ignored the absence of Tomás, the impenetrability of the jungle, my mother’s warning.

“We?” I asked.

“Of course. I must document this. And you… you can read the site. As you read the text. You can see what the mirror cannot be brought to see.”

The idea was monstrous. To go to the source of the unraveling, to the raw wound in the world that had created León’s condition. To take my own fraying thread and dangle it over the abyss.

“No,” I said.

“Catalina, this is history breathing!” my father cried. “This is the pulse of the real!”

“It is the pulse of a curse! And Mother—”

“Your mother will be fine. Rosa from the village can sit with her for a day. Two days at most.”

León was silent, watching our conflict. He would not ask me to go. He would not have to. The pull was already there, in my name on that skin-page. A hook in my soul.

I thought of my life at La Ceniza: the slow decay, the whispering house, the river taking and giving back, my father digging in the past, my mother fading from the present. It was a life of waiting. Waiting for the roof to fall, for the river to flood, for my mother to forget my name. Was this not just another form of unraveling?

“If I go,” I said, my voice hollow, “it is not for your history, Father. And it is not for your ghost, León Sombra. It is for the name in that book. I go to ask it why it knows me.”

The decision was made. The machinery of preparation clanked to life. My father packed his maps, a compass, water, his pistol “for snakes.” León changed into sturdy boots and borrowed a bush knife from a shed. I packed nothing but a canteen and a deep, foreboding dread.

We left at first light the next day. Rosa, a broad-faced woman with kind eyes, sat on our veranda with her knitting, promising to watch over my mother. My mother herself did not come to see us off. She stood at her bedroom window, a pale figure behind the glass, her hand pressed to the pane. A final, silent warning.

Tomás’s boat, the *María Sangrante*, was found tied up at his rickety dock downstream. The old man was not there, but the key was in the ignition. His absence felt like an omen, but my father dismissed it. “He’s probably drunk in Puerto Viejo. We’ll leave him payment.”

The boat puttered upstream, the jungle closing in on both sides like green walls. The world shrank to the width of the river, the growl of the engine, the scream of unseen birds. The air grew thicker, hotter, full of the scent of rotting vegetation and something else—a metallic tang, like old blood.

After three hours, my father pointed to a narrow, overgrown creek mouth on the left. “The Río Sombra,” he announced. “It gets shallow. We walk from here.”

We tied the boat to a strangler fig and entered the jungle. It was not a walk; it was a swim through solid green. León led, hacking with the machete. My father followed, compass in hand. I brought up the rear, the silence of the deep jungle pressing on my ears. It was a silence that was not empty, but full—of clicks, of rustles, of the drip of water from a thousand leaves.

We found the Stone Lion just before the light began to fail. It was not a lion. It was a massive, weathered outcrop of black rock that, from one angle, in a certain slant of greenish light, *might* have suggested a crouching beast with a heavy head. It crouched at the precise point where two streams met—one dark and slow (the Sombra), one clearer and faster (the Espíritu). The water swirled together in a pool at the lion’s feet, a vortex of conflicting currents.

The confluence.

The air here was different. Colder. The constant insect hum of the jungle was absent. It was silent, as if the world was holding its breath. The light seemed to pool in strange ways, making shadows that didn’t match the trees.

“This is it,” my father whispered, his bravado gone, replaced by awe.

León stood at the water’s edge, staring into the swirling pool. His face was a mask of anguish and recognition. “I can feel it,” he said, his voice raw. “The echo is loud here. It’s… a screaming in my blood.”

He took a step into the water.

“León, wait—” I started.

But he kept going, wading into the pool up to his knees, his waist. He was drawn, hypnotized. At the center of the confluence, where the waters fought, he stopped. He closed his eyes.

And then, he began to change.

Not his form, but his… *substance*. He seemed to flicker, like a candle flame in a draft. For a second, I saw two of him—one solid, one a transparent, greyish overlay, facing a different direction, its mouth open in a silent shout. The air crackled with that ozone smell.

“The veil,” my father breathed, fumbling for his notebook.

“León!” I shouted, splashing into the cold water after him. “Get out of there!”

He didn’t hear me. The overlay—his echo, his ghost-twin—was becoming clearer. It was reaching for something in the water, something only it could see.

I grabbed his arm. His flesh was icy cold. He turned his head, and his eyes were not his own. They were full of a centuries-old terror. The eyes of Don Álvaro.

“*La Tejedora*,” the thing inside him hissed, its voice a dry rustle of leaves and old paper. “*She is not in the box. She is the box. The weaver is the weave.*”

Then it—he—screamed. A sound of pure, dimensional agony. The overlay snapped into perfect alignment with León’s body for a horrific instant, and then *pulled*.

It was not pulling León. It was pulling *from* him. Pulling from the water, from the air, from the very stone.

It was pulling threads.

I saw them. Faint, luminous filaments, like spider silk charged with moonlight, erupting from the pool, from the jungle, from *me*. They were the threads of possibility, the unchosen lives, the echoes. And they were all snagging, tangling, drawing toward the vortex where León stood.

My father cried out, clutching his chest. A thin, silver filament was stretching from his sternum toward the water. I looked down and saw one emerging from my own heart, a cold, taut line of light connecting me to the maelstrom.

León’s echo had not come to commune. It had come to *weave*. To finish what Don Álvaro started. To gather the frayed threads and, in a final, catastrophic act of selfish magic, re-knot them all into a single, unbroken cord for itself.

The curse was not a passive haunting. It was a machine. And we had just provided the final, living parts.

The pool began to boil with stolen light. The Stone Lion seemed to lean forward, ready to pounce. And León, my ghost with the quartz eyes, was the loom.



---

# Chapter 4

## **Chapter 4**

The threads were not metaphors. They were physics. A cold, crystalline mathematics of the soul, and they were unspooling me.

I felt it as a visceral tug, a fishhook set deep behind my breastbone, reeling in a line of light I had not known I contained. The scream that followed was not León’s, nor the dry rustle of Don Álvaro’s stolen voice. It was the air itself, the water, the ancient stone, shrieking as their latent histories were ripped free. The world was being plucked like a harp strung with ghosts, and every note was a life that might have been.

My father collapsed to his knees on the wet stone, one hand clawing at the silver filament stretching taut from his chest. His notebook tumbled into the churning pool, the pages blooming with dark water before they were swallowed. His face was not pained, but astonished—the raw wonder of a scholar witnessing a fundamental law crack open before him. Even here, even now.

“Catalina,” he gasped. “The threads… they’re not just echoes. They’re *choices*. The unmade ones.”

I understood. The overlay—León’s ghost-twin, Don Álvaro’s desperate, hungry will—was not gathering memories. It was gathering *potential*. All the branching paths León had never walked, all the loves he had never nursed, all the deaths he had never died. It was a loom of absences, and it was weaving a cord of pure, singular existence. A life without divergence. A soul without shadow. The ultimate, monstrous act of self-preservation.

I lunged for León, my own filament burning cold as it stretched. The water around his legs was no longer water. It was a broth of phosphorescent strands, a nest of seething, possible lives. His physical body was rigid, a lightning rod. His eyes, those quartz-chip eyes I had traced in the dark of my mind for years, were wide with a terror that was entirely his own. The overlay had aligned, but it had not fully displaced him. He was trapped behind his own skin, watching the machine of the curse use him as its spindle.

I grabbed his face. His skin was the temperature of the deep earth.
“León! Look at me!”
The overlay flickered across his features—the sharper jaw of Don Álvaro, the gaunt hollows of a 17th-century nobleman starving for time. But beneath it, like a face under ice, I saw him straining.
“*La Tejedora*,” the rustling voice hissed again from his throat. “*The weaver is the weave. To mend the tapestry, one must become the thread.*”
“Let him go,” I snarled, my voice foreign, guttural.
“*He is the thread. The final thread. The love that did not die. The anchor point.*”

Love. The word, in that desiccated voice, was the most obscene thing I had ever heard. It was not an emotion. It was a tool, a snare, a knot in the metaphysical fabric they had exploited. My love—my stupid, tenacious, graveyard love—was the fixed point they were using to reel all the rest in.

The Stone Lion’s maw seemed to widen. From its throat issued not a roar, but a deep, subsonic *hum*, the sound a planet might make turning on an axis of grief. The filaments brightened, their pull intensifying. I felt a dizzying, unraveling sensation. Not my body, but my *self*. A memory surfaced, unbidden and sharp: not of León, but of a morning two years after he vanished. I stood at the riverbank where my mother lost her shoes. I held a smooth, grey stone. I thought, *I could skip this stone, or I could throw it as far as I can, or I could drop it here and walk away*. I had dropped it. In that moment, I saw the stone skimming the brown water, saw it sinking into the deep, silent channel. I saw both. The unmade choice glimmered beside the real one, and now, that ghost-stone was a thread of light, yanked from the sediment of my past toward León’s vortex.

This was the machine. It harvested regret.

“Papa!” I cried, still holding León’s frozen face. “How do we stop it?”

My father was on all fours, breathing in ragged, scientific gusts. “The weave… needs a pattern! A design! It’s not just gathering—it’s *sorting*. Looking for the correct configuration…” He looked up, water streaming from his hair and glasses. His own filament was now as thick as a pencil, a solid beam of stolen possibility. “It needs a weaver’s intention! Without that, it’s just… chaos.”

As if on cue, the thing inside León raised his right hand. The movement was jerky, puppet-like. His fingers, pale and elegant, began to move in the air. Not randomly. They *plucked*. They *twisted*. They *tied*.

With each motion, a cluster of distant threads would snap taut and braid together with a sound like ringing glass. A new cord formed, thicker, brighter, humming with a coherent purpose. It slithered through the air toward the Stone Lion’s open mouth. The lion was not the source. It was the *shuttle*. The vessel for the newly woven history.

The pain changed. It was no longer just a pull. It was a *re-writing*. A sharp, localized agony bloomed in my left knee—the memory of a fall from a horse I never rode. The taste of licorice, which I despise, flooded my mouth—the ghost of a sweetheart who never was. I was being edited. My unchosen lives were being culled, sorted, and stitched into Don Álvaro’s grand, selfish tapestry.

I did the only thing I could think of. I stopped trying to pull León back. I stepped into the vortex.

The cold was absolute. It was the cold of the void between stars, of time before life. It seared my lungs. The light was blinding—not a glare, but a supernova of narratives, each filament a brilliant, screaming biography. I wrapped my arms around León, pressing my forehead against his. Our two silver threads, now side-by-side, thrummed like violin strings.

“You are not a thread,” I whispered, my voice lost in the cacophony. “You are León. You hated licorice. You had a scar on your thumb from a fishing knife. You laughed with your whole body.”

The plucking fingers hesitated.

“You are not a loom.” I was crying now, the tears freezing on my cheeks. “You are the boy who watched the river with me. You are my ghost. And I am keeping you.”

I kissed him. It was not a kiss of romance. It was a claim. A seal. A defiance written in the oldest language. His lips were like marble, but beneath them, I felt a tremor. A fracture in the overlay’s control.

The rustling voice erupted, furious. “*The anchor must hold! The pattern must be made!*”

But the pattern was breaking. My father, with a strength I didn’t know he possessed, had staggered to his feet. He wasn’t pulling against his thread anymore. He was *following* it, walking deliberately along its line like a tightrope walker, his arms outstretched for balance. He was mumbling, not incantations, but taxonomy. “Fractal recursion… sympathetic resonance… the observer effect…”

He reached the edge of the boiling pool, directly in front of the Stone Lion. He looked at the beast, at the braided cord of light snaking into its mouth, and he nodded, as if confirming a hypothesis.

Then he took off his waterlogged glasses, and with a calm, deliberate motion, he threw them into the lion’s maw.

They were just glass and wire. A trivial thing. But they were *his*. The lens through which he saw, measured, and ordered the world. His act of observation.

The effect was instantaneous and catastrophic. The braided cord of light, the first completed length of Don Álvaro’s new history, touched the foreign object—the un-theorized, personal artifact. The pattern rejected it. The cord thrashed like a speared eel, then exploded into a thousand frayed ends.

The backlash was a silent, concussive wave of disorganized possibility. It blew outwards from the lion.

The overlay over León shattered like a pane of glass. He and the ghost-twin fell out of alignment. For a second, I saw both clearly: León, gasping, returning to his own eyes; and Don Álvaro’s echo, a transparent, ravenous figure of dust and old light, its hands still clutching at empty air where the threads had been.

Then the echo blew apart into motes of luminescent dust.

The pull ceased. The filaments winked out, one by one, their light draining back into the world, into the unseen strata of what-might-have-been. The hum died. The pool settled into mere water, dark and littered with dead leaves.

The silence was heavier than the noise had been.

León sagged against me, a dead weight. I stumbled back, dragging him out of the water, onto the moss. He was breathing—shallow, ragged gulps. He was real. He was solid. He was *here*.

My father sat down hard on the stone, staring at the placid mouth of the lion. He looked ten years older. The filament was gone from his chest, but a faint, silver weal remained, like a scar from an invisible burn.

I checked León’s pulse. It beat against my fingers, a frantic, living rhythm. His eyes fluttered open. They were his own. Just his. Filled with confusion, with residual terror, and with a dawning, impossible recognition.

“Cat?” he whispered. The name was a breath, a broken thing.

The world did not right itself. It simply stopped ending. The jungle exhaled a damp, rotting sigh. The ozone smell faded, replaced by the familiar perfume of wet earth and decay. We were three survivors on a tiny stage of stone, surrounded by a night that had witnessed too much.

We did not speak on the journey back. Words were too small, too new. We moved through the jungle like sleepwalkers, my father leading with a stunned, automatic precision, me half-carrying León, whose legs were unreliable. He leaned into me, his warmth a shocking contrast to the earlier cold. Every few steps, he would look at me, his eyes searching my face as if reading a text in a forgotten language.

La Ceniza awaited us, a darker blot in the dark. The house did not feel like a sanctuary. It felt like a cocoon of older, slower consequences. We got León to the threadbare sofa in the main room. My father lit a single kerosene lamp, its yellow light painting our faces into hollowed masks. He brought blankets, a bottle of aguardiente. The practicalities were a lifeline.

León drank from the bottle, coughed, and finally found his voice. It was hoarse, eroded.
“I was… aware. The whole time. It was like being locked in a glass coffin, watching your own hands betray you.” He looked at his hands now as if they were foreign. “He was so… *lonely*. A centuries-deep loneliness. It wasn’t malice. It was hunger. A starving man will eat anything.”

“He was trying to weave himself a new life,” my father said, his voice devoid of its usual lecture-hall cadence. It was flat, awestruck. “Using the untapped potential of your bloodline. Using the unresolved… attachment.” He couldn’t look at me when he said it. “The curse was never a haunting. It was an engine. A metaphysical loom, waiting for the right components. Your mother’s sensitivity. My… academic obsession. Catalina’s…” He trailed off.

“My anchor,” I finished for him, the word tasting of iron.

León’s eyes found mine. “You stepped into it. Why?”

“There was nowhere else to go.”

He held my gaze, and in that look, the years collapsed. He was not the phantom I’d mourned, nor the tragic hero of a ghost story. He was a man who had been used as a tool by a dead aristocrat, who was shivering under a scratchy blanket, who was terrified. The love I had carried was a monument. What I felt now was something sharper, more immediate: a fierce and desperate need to protect this fragile, returned reality of him.

My father cleared his throat, the scholar reasserting itself over the shell-shocked man. “This changes everything. The ‘echoes’ are not mere recordings. They are *residual wills*. Intentions trapped in the fabric of this place. Don Álvaro’s was the strongest, the most coherent—a singular, monstrous desire to live a single, perfect life. But if his exists…” He looked from me to León, his meaning clear.

There were other hungers in the stone.

***

The next three days were a tense, fragile apprenticeship. We were not explorer and guide anymore. We were a core. A trinity forged in the backlash of a metaphysical machine.

My father became the Theorist. He spent hours at the heavy desk, scribbling on fresh paper, mapping the events at the pool with diagrams that looked like a mad physicist’s work. He spoke of “temporal eddies,” “psychic resonance,” and “the permeability of historical events.” His lens was logic, a scaffold to hang the nightmare on. His role was to understand the rules of the world that had been revealed.

“The echoes are drawn to emotional resonance,” he said one morning, tapping his notes. “Don Álvaro fixated on León because of the bloodline, yes, but also because of the *unfinished* quality of his disappearance. A life cut off is a powerful lure. It’s a loose thread. They want to tie it off—for their own purposes.”

León became the Relic. He was our living reference book, our witness from inside the storm. His memories of the overlay were traumatic, but precise. He could describe the sensation of the threads—not as light, but as “currents of feeling.” He recalled snippets of Don Álvaro’s fractured consciousness: a memory of a courtyard in Spain, the smell of orange blossoms, the crushing weight of a debt that could not be paid. His role was to provide the texture, the subjective truth of the curse. He was also, quietly, recovering. The simple acts—eating soup, feeling sunlight on his skin, sleeping in a real bed—were revelations. I often caught him just staring at his own hands, flexing his fingers, marveling at their obedience.

And I became the Anchor. It was not a title I wanted. It was a function I could not deny. My father’s theory was clear: my persistent connection to León, my refusal to let his story end, had been the stable point the curse exploited. But that same stubbornness had also been the crack in Don Álvaro’s design. My love was the flaw in the machine. Now, I had to learn to wield that not as a passive state, but as a deliberate tool. A grounding wire.

We tested it, cautiously, in the overgrown garden. León would stand at one end, I at the other. My father would watch with his now-unassisted eyes (he refused to get new glasses, calling the old ones “a necessary sacrifice”). I would focus on León—not on the ghost he had been, but on the man before me. The scar on his thumb. The way he pushed his hair back when he was thinking. The specific, mundane reality of him. And I would feel… a steadiness. A quiet hum in the air between us. When my father brought out a small, charged relic—a rusted nail from the original hacienda foundation—the hum would intensify, and the nail’s faint, whispering echo would dim, as if muffled.

“You are a dampening field, Catalina,” my father said, a note of pride in his voice. “A localized negation of their influence. For them, you are the embodiment of the ‘real,’ the ‘chosen’ path. It is anathema to their nature as ‘might-have-beens.’”

It was a power, of a sort. It felt less like a power and more like a burden. To be the anchor is to be the thing that does not move, that holds fast while the storm tears everything else away. It is a lonely station.

Our core was solidifying, but the house itself seemed to resent it. La Ceniza, The Ashes, began to live up to its name. The antagonism was not direct, not a face in the window. It was symbolic, insidious.

On the second day, all the clocks stopped at 4:17, the hour of my mother’s death. Not just the grandfather clock in the hall, but my father’s pocket watch, the digital display on a forgotten radio in the attic. They all died at that same, silent moment.

On the third day, we found the portrait of my great-grandmother in the dining room turned face to the wall. When we righted it, her eyes in the painting had been meticulously scratched out, not with a knife, but with something finer, like a needle. The empty sockets seemed to weep dust.

That night, the smell of orange blossoms filled the house, thick and cloying. Don Álvaro’s memory-smell. It was a scent of profound, homesick longing. It made León retch. It made my father’s eyes water with a grief that wasn’t his own. For me, it was a taunt. A reminder that the weaver was gone, but the weave remained. The pattern of his longing was still embedded in the house, in the land.

The subordinate antagonist was the house itself. It was a subordinate to the greater, lingering will of the land, acting out the residual echoes of all the other hungers Don Álvaro had stirred up. It was testing us. Probing the strength of our new formation.

We should have been more careful. We should have heeded the warnings.

But we were emboldened by our small successes. We had faced the master echo and survived. We had a theory, a team, a tool. My father, in particular, was driven by a feverish curiosity. The setback came because of this—because of his foundational flaw, the scholar’s hubris that believes everything can be known, can be catalogued, if one just looks closely enough.

He decided we needed to map the “epicenter.” The pool was the loom’s location, but the source of the energy, he postulated, was older. It had to be the original site of Don Álvaro’s sin, the place where he first performed the magic that bound his fate to this land. He believed he could find it in his notes, in the historical plat maps, cross-referenced with León’s sensory memories.

“If we can find the source,” he said, his eyes alight with that dangerous, intellectual fire, “we might be able to deactivate the resonance at its root. Not just fight the symptoms, but cure the disease.”

León was wary. “The closer we get to that… the hungrier they will become. It’s not just him anymore. I can feel them. The others. They’re faint, but they’re… attentive.”

I sided with my father. I was tired of being reactive. Tired of the scratching in the walls, the stopped clocks, the ghost of orange blossoms. I wanted to find the heart and drive a stake through it. My flaw was not curiosity, but impatience. A desire to end the story, to secure the fragile reality of León’s return, to have a life that was not dictated by the griefs of dead men.

So, on the fourth morning, we went back into the jungle. Not to the pool, but deeper, following my father’s pieced-together map and León’s increasingly uneasy intuition. The green light grew dimmer, the air thicker. We were moving into a part of the land that felt not just wild, but *sealed*. The birdsong ceased. The only sound was the squelch of our boots in the perpetual damp and the low, worried rhythm of our breathing.

We found it at the base of a limestone cliff, veiled by a curtain of strangler figs. Not a ruin. A *seal*. The stones were blackened, not by fire, but by something older than fire. They were arranged in a perfect circle, and in the center was a depression—not a pool, but a dry, stone basin. Carved around its rim was a single, repeating phrase in archaic Spanish, worn smooth by time but still legible: *Lo Que Se Da, No Se Quita*. What Is Given, Cannot Be Taken.

This was the altar. The place of the original transaction.

The air here was not charged. It was *empty*. A vacuum. It sucked the sound, the warmth, the hope right out of you.

“This is it,” my father whispered, his voice absorbed by the stone. He stepped toward the basin, his hand reaching for his notebook.

“Wait,” León said, his voice strained. “Can’t you feel it? It’s not a source. It’s a *mouth*. It’s not giving out energy. It’s… waiting.”

But it was too late. My father’s boot scuffed a flat stone at the edge of the circle. It tilted with a gritty sigh.

The world did not erupt. It inverted.

The emptiness in the circle became absolute. The light didn’t fade; it was *eaten*. From the dry basin, something poured forth. Not water. Not light. *Silence*. A wave of pure, negation sound that hit us like a physical wall. It was the silence of the grave, of the void before creation, of every promise ever broken.

And with it came the cold. The same cold from the vortex.

This was not Don Álvaro’s work. This was older. This was the counter-weight. The curse had a counterpart: the *price*. *Lo Que Se Da, No Se Quita*. Don Álvaro had given something here to power his selfish magic. And what he had given was now eternally, hungrily absent.

The silence swallowed my father’s cry. It swallowed my shout for León. I saw them both, their forms bleached of color, of detail, like figures in a faded photograph. They were moving in slow motion, fighting against the tide of nothingness.

My anchor instinct flared. I focused on León, on the real, on the chosen. The hum rose in me, a tiny bell ringing in a vast cathedral of quiet. It pushed the silence back a foot, just around me. A tiny pocket of reality.

But I couldn’t reach them both. The effort to hold my own space was like holding up a collapsing ceiling. I took a step toward my father, who was closer, his glasses-less eyes wide with a terrible, final understanding. He wasn’t trying to run. He was staring into the basin, seeing the truth of his theory, and it was costing him everything.

I stretched out my hand. Our fingertips were an inch apart.

And then the silence *pulled*.

It didn’t pull him. It pulled the ground from under him. The limestone at his feet, weathered and fragile, crumbled. Not in a crash, but in a whisper of dissolving stone.

He didn’t make a sound as he vanished into the fissure that opened up, swallowed by the blackness and the silence. One moment he was there, on the brink of revelation. The next, he was gone. The fissure sealed itself with a soft, final sigh, leaving only unbroken stone.

The wave of negation receded as suddenly as it had come. Sound rushed back—the drip of water, the rustle of leaves, the raw, ragged sound of my own screaming sobs. The green light of the jungle was blindingly, mockingly vibrant.

León stumbled to the now-featureless circle, falling to his knees where the fissure had been. He pounded on the stone. It was solid, ancient, implacable.

I stood frozen, my hand still outstretched. The anchor had held—but only for me. I had secured my own reality, and in doing so, I had failed to extend it to the one who needed it most. My impatience, my desire for a clean ending, had led us here. My father’s curiosity had found the source, and the source had taken its due.

*What Is Given, Cannot Be Taken.*

Don Álvaro had given a life to power his loom of stolen potential. And now, the altar had claimed another to maintain the balance. A life for a life. A thread for a thread.

The house’s petty hauntings had been a distraction. This was the true influence of the antagonist—not a face, not a ghost, but the relentless, mathematical cruelty of the curse itself. It had used our own resolve, our own strengths, against us. It had lured the theorist with the promise of knowledge and consumed him for it.

León looked up at me from the ground, his face a mask of ash and despair. In his eyes, I saw no accusation. Only a shared, bottomless horror. We were the core now, halved. The theorist was gone. Only the relic and the anchor remained, stranded at the mouth of a silence that had just taken everything.

And as the jungle heat pressed in on us, a new, more terrible understanding dawned. The altar was satisfied. For now. But the machine was still intact. The loom remained. And we were down to two living parts.

The hook was set deep. It was no longer in my heart, but in the fabric of everything. We had gone to cure the disease and had instead fed it a vital piece of ourselves. The rules of this world were not just strange; they were predatory. And our next move would not be an exploration. It would be a rescue mission into a place that traded in absences, or a desperate, likely doomed, attempt to dismantle the engine of the curse with half its required components missing.

The river was a slow, brown god. It took things and did not give them back. And we were standing in its deepest, darkest current.



---

# Chapter 5

## **Chapter 5**

The house, La Ceniza, no longer breathed. It held its breath. The silence it exuded was not the quiet of absence, but the dense, woolen hush of a cathedral after the last note of a requiem has died. It was a silence you had to push through, like deep water. León and I moved through its rooms as if we were its final, unwelcome ghosts.

My father’s study was a fossil. The air was sharp with the scent of ozone and old paper, the ghost of his pipe tobacco lingering like a memory too stubborn to fade. His loom of theories—the maps, the string-laden pinboards, the open journals with his frantic, elegant script—sat frozen. A machine without its engineer. I ran a finger along the edge of his desk. The dust there was new. It had settled in the hours since his absence, a soft, grey shroud. The anchor in my chest, that cold, familiar stone, felt heavier than ever. It had held me fast to the earth while the current took him. A life for a life. I was the relic left on the bank.

León stood in the doorway, a silhouette against the gloom of the hall. He had washed the altar’s ash from his face, but it lingered in the lines around his eyes, in the grim set of his mouth. He was not a man built for stillness. His energy usually hummed in a room, a low voltage. Now, he was a doused lamp.

“He knew the risk,” León said. His voice was gravel, worn smooth by the river of the last twenty-four hours. “The theorist always knows. He steps into the equation.”

“He was my father,” I said. The words were simple, stupid. They hung in the charged air, inadequate as a leaf in a storm.

“I know.” León entered the room, his eyes avoiding the empty chair. He went to the largest map, the one that showed the valley, the river’s lazy curve, and the dense knot of topography where the old Hacienda de la Tejedora—the Weaver’s Estate—was marked with a black ‘X’. My father’s final conjecture. The source. The cave. “His knowing doesn’t make the taking right. It just makes it part of the pattern.”

*What Is Given, Cannot Be Taken.* The curse’s first law. But my father hadn’t given himself. He had been taken, his curiosity the bait, the altar’s hunger the hook. The rules were predatory, yes, but they were also a perverse logic. A loom needed thread. A pattern required sacrifice.

“We go there,” I said. It wasn’t a question.

“We go there,” León confirmed. “Not to explore. To dismantle. To unweave.”

“With half the components missing.”

“Then we will be blunt,” he said, a flicker of his old fire returning, cold and blue. “If we cannot operate the machine, we break its teeth.”

The plan was a fragile thing, spun from the remnants of my father’s notes and our own desperate intuition. The Hacienda de la Tejedora was not just a place, but the conceptual heart of Don Álvaro’s legacy. The physical location of his original loom, the seat of his power. If the curse was a pattern woven into the land, then the loom was the shuttle that continued its motion. To stop it, we had to reach it. My father believed the entrance was not through a door, but through a convergence—a place where the land’s memory was thin, where one could push through from one layer of the curse to another. He had identified it: a cave system near the old hacienda’s ruins, where the river dove underground.

We were to be the needle, piercing the fabric.

***

Old Tomás found us at the riverbank at dawn. He was not three days late this time; he was an apparition summoned by our need, his flat-bottomed skiff materializing from the mist that coiled on the brown water like smoke. His face was a topography of wrinkles, his eyes the color of wet stone.

“La Ceniza is lighter,” he said by way of greeting, his gaze moving from me to the silent house behind us. “It sits higher on the hill. A soul-weight is gone.”

I flinched. León loaded the last of our packs into the boat—minimal supplies, my father’s journals, the cold iron of our resolve.

“You know where we go,” I said.

“I know the water that goes there,” Tomás corrected, poling the skiff away from the bank. The river accepted us with a soft, sucking sound. “I know where it goes quiet and dark. I do not follow it. No one who values their breath follows it.”

The journey was a silent, upstream penitence. The jungle on either side was a wall of breathing green. Parrots shrieked, their cries like tearing metal. The light was the color of weak tea. I watched the water, this slow, brown god. It had taken my mother’s shoes. It had taken my father. It did not give things back. It only carried them to its secret places.

León sat amidships, his hands resting on his knees. He was checking and re-checking our pitiful arsenal in his mind: salt, iron filings, candles of pure beeswax, a silver whistle that was supposed to disrupt harmonic resonances, my own anchoring will. It was a theorist’s kit, missing its theorist.

“He would tell us we are underprepared,” León said, not looking at me.

“He did tell us,” I replied, remembering the frantic scribbles in the margin of his final journal entry. *‘The loom weaves with potential. It will use your own strength, your own love, as its thread. To approach it is to offer yourself up to the pattern. The only defense is a will that refuses to be shaped.’* I had read it as a warning. Now it read like an epitaph.

Old Tomás poled us into a tributary, narrower, the trees leaning in so close their roots clawed at the water like skeletal hands. The air grew cooler, damper. The sound of the main river faded, replaced by the drip of moisture from leaves and the low, subterranean groan of water moving through stone.

“Here,” Tomás said finally, nudging the skiff against a muddy bank where the limestone cliff face was cracked open in a dark, vertical slit. It was not a cave mouth, but a wound. “The water goes in there. The breath of the old place comes out. You feel it?”

I did. It was a draft, not of air, but of feeling. A sigh of profound, weary loneliness, tinged with a metallic hunger. It was the exhalation of the machine.

We disembarked. Tomás did not wish us luck. He simply looked at us, his wet-stone eyes holding a depth of pity that was worse than fear. “The river brings things down,” he said. “It rarely brings them up. Remember which way is flow.”

Then he and his skiff were gone, swallowed by the green tunnel, leaving us on the bank of that sighing stone.

***

The conflict did not surface as a shout. It surfaced as a fracture in the silence.

Inside the cave’s mouth, the world turned inside out. The heat died, replaced by a chill that clung to the bones. Our flashlight beams were pathetic things, carving small, trembling islands of visibility out of a vast, dripping dark. The walls were not stone, but a conglomerate of root and rock, of fossilized wood and something that glistened like obsidian. It felt less like a geological formation and more like a scar, slowly healing over something that did not belong.

We made camp in a chamber where the ceiling soared into blackness. The sound of the underground river was a constant, whispering presence, a thread of sound in the stillness. As León methodically laid out a ring of salt and iron, I sat on my bedroll, my father’s journal open in my lap.

The words blurred. All I could see was his hand, the way he held his pen, the slight tremor of excitement when he was on the verge of a insight. That hand was gone. Taken. Reduced to a component. The cold anchor in my chest throbbed, a second, sterile heart. I had anchored myself. I had let him go. Was that the failure? Or was the failure believing I could have held him, that my love was a rope strong enough to haul him back from a metaphysical current?

“It will use your own love as its thread.”

León’s voice, quiet, came from across the salt ring. He was watching me, his face carved by the stark flashlight shadows. “You are thinking you could have swapped places.”

I didn’t answer.

“You couldn’t,” he said, not unkindly. “The anchor is yours. It is a part of your… signature. Your father was the theorist. He was the one who sought the source. The curse took what was offered. Not what was held back.”

“I didn’t offer him.”

“You didn’t have to. He offered himself. The moment he dedicated his life to understanding this place, he entered the transaction.” León poked at the small, smokeless fire of beeswax candles he’d lit. “Love mortality, Alba.”

I looked up sharply. The term was my father’s. From his private lexicon.

León saw my surprise. “He talked to me, too. The night before we went to the altar. He said the most powerful curses are not fueled by hate, but by a corrupted love. A love that fears mortality so deeply it seeks to cheat it, to weave the beloved into the tapestry of the world so they can never be lost. Don Álvaro didn’t just want power. He wanted to preserve. To make permanent. That’s what the loom is. A desperate, frozen love. Its mortality is its hunger. It must consume to sustain what it has preserved.”

The words fell into the silence of the cave, finding their place like stones in a pool. Love mortality. The fear of loss so great it distorts into a kind of consumption. To keep something forever, you must make it a part of the system. You must feed it.

My love for my father was a sharp, clean pain. It was a missing limb. It was not hungry. It was simply absent. But the curse… the curse was love turned necrotic. It was the Weaver’s love for his legacy, fossilized into a mechanism that ate lives to keep its memory alive.

“We are not here to fight a ghost,” I whispered, the understanding dawning like a cold, new star. “We are here to euthanize a love that forgot how to die.”

León nodded, once. The old conflict between us—his pragmatism, my inherited obsession—did not surface. It had been burned away in the altar’s ash. What remained was a shared, terrible purpose. And a fear.

“My fear,” León said, voicing it, “is that we will see him. Your father. Woven into the pattern. A thread in the tapestry. And that my resolve will not survive the sight.”

It was the fear we had not named. That my father was not gone, but *repurposed*. That we would find him there, a screaming filament in the grand, terrible design. A fate worse than death: eternal, conscious utility.

“If we see him,” I said, my voice strangely calm, “then we know the machine is still working. And we will know what we have to break.”

The confession hung between us. The ultimate blasphemy against love: to choose the mercy of true death over the obscenity of preserved existence. It was the shattering of expectation. This was not a rescue. It was a severing.

***

The doubt did not come as a storm. It came as the river’s whisper, growing louder as we descended.

We followed the water, a black ribbon glistening in our light, deeper into the mountain’s gut. The passages narrowed, then opened into caverns where stalactites dripped with a slow, eternal patience. The air grew thicker, carrying a scent now—not damp earth, but ozone, old wool, and a faint, coppery tang.

In a vast chamber, the river pooled before disappearing under a wall of seamless, dark rock. The wall was not natural. It was too smooth, too vertical. Carved into its surface was a single, massive emblem: a circle interlaced with a vertical loom, the warp threads stretching to infinity, the weft thread a single, unbroken line that spiraled inwards. The symbol of the Tejedora.

This was the threshold. The convergence.

My anchor stone felt like a block of ice. It was pulling me *down*, toward the wall, as if it recognized its opposite pole. This was the heart of the pattern that had shaped my life, that had drawn my mother to the riverbank, that had consumed my father. My will, which had always been a quiet, stubborn flame, guttered. What was I? A woman with a cold stone in her chest and a head full of other people’s theories. I was not a warrior. I was a librarian of the uncanny. My tools were observation and endurance, not demolition.

I had failed to extend my anchor. I had failed to save him. What arrogance made me think I could unravel the central knot of a century-old curse?

I placed my hand against the carved symbol. The stone was not cold. It was warm, almost pulsing. A faint, rhythmic *thrum* traveled up my arm, a vibration like a distant, gigantic engine. I could feel the loom working, somewhere beyond this door. Weaving. Consuming.

León came to stand beside me. He didn’t speak. He simply waited. He was the relic, the object that had endured. His strength was not in action, but in *being*. In having witnessed the worst and remaining intact.

I thought of my mother’s shoes, swallowed without a sound. I thought of my father’s final, eager glance before he stepped into the altar’s geometry. They had been taken by the current. I had always been on the bank, holding the ghost of a teacup, watching.

“I am tired of the bank,” I said, my voice echoing strangely in the chamber.

León understood. “Then we enter the current.”

Our final preparation was absurdly simple. We had no grand ritual. We extinguished all but one candle. In that fragile light, I focused not on the anchor’s cold weight, but on the emptiness it surrounded. The clean, sharp absence of my father. The quiet love for my mother, which was not a hunger, but a memory. I held the feeling of loss, not as a weakness, but as a truth. A mortal truth.

Love mortality. To love is to accept loss. The curse was the refusal. The loom was the refusal made manifest.

I took the silver whistle and blew. It made no audible sound, but the air in the chamber *shivered*. The *thrum* in the wall stuttered. León, in the same moment, threw a handful of iron filings at the center of the carved loom.

The filings did not fall. They stuck to the stone, sizzling, tracing the spiral of the weft thread. The stone within the spiral darkened, then dissolved into a patch of absolute blackness—a doorway not through rock, but through the world itself.

The draft that came from it was no sigh. It was a inhalation. It pulled at our clothes, our hair, the very light from our candle. It was the breath of the machine.

We looked at each other, our faces drawn and ancient in the guttering light. No more words. We had voiced our fears, confessed our doubts, and named our terrible purpose. We had shattered the expectation of a heroic rescue and embraced a grimmer, more final duty.

Together, we stepped through the black spiral, into the heart of the refusal.

***

The other side was not a cave.

It was the belly of the loom.

We stood on a narrow stone walkway, overlooking a chasm of impossible scale. The air was hot and dry, humming with a deafening, rhythmic *CLACK-CLACK-CLACK*. Stretching into the luminous, amber-hazed distance were the frames of countless, gigantic looms. They were not wood and thread, but structures of polished bone and strands of solidified light, of liquid shadow, of shimmering potential. They rose and fell in a monstrous, synchronized ballet, weaving a tapestry that was not cloth, but the very substance of the valley above—the green of a leaf, the brown of the river, the grey of La Ceniza’s stones, the fleeting gold of a sunset.

And the threads… the threads were lives.

I saw them, glowing filaments fed into the monstrous shuttles. Some were bright and new, screaming with recent terror—the lost hikers, the vanished farmers. Others were dim, almost exhausted, their memories and vitality nearly spent. The loom took them, wove their essence into the pattern of the land, and in doing so, sustained the timeless, static perfection of Don Álvaro’s legacy. A valley preserved in amber, fed by stolen time.

The walkway led to a central platform, where the largest loom of all stood. Its frame was black iron, its warp threads were veins of molten gold, and its weft was a single, pulsing ribbon of deep crimson. Before it, his back to us, stood a figure. Not a ghost, but a memory so dense it had achieved solidity. Don Álvaro. The Weaver. He moved with a slow, deliberate grace, guiding the crimson thread, his focus absolute.

And there, feeding into the shuttle of that central loom, was a thread that was not bright with terror, but steady with intellectual passion, with a fierce, loving curiosity. A thread that glowed with a familiar, achingly dear light.

My father.

He was not screaming. He was intent, fascinated, his essence utterly absorbed in the beautiful, terrible mechanics of the pattern. He had found his source. He was part of it now. A theorist, woven into the theory.

The hook was not just set in the fabric of everything. It was here, in my heart, tearing it open. León’s hand clamped on my arm, his grip iron, holding me back from a cry, from a run forward into the consuming machinery.

This was the heart of the antagonist’s power. Not a man, but a memory. Not a ghost, but a love that had murdered its own object in order to keep it. A love that had become a machine, and a machine that had become a world.

We had crossed the threshold. We stood in the engine room of mortality denied.

And the Weaver, sensing a new, unaccounted-for potential, began to turn.



---

# Chapter 6

## **Chapter 6**

The Weaver turned, and the world turned with him.

It was not the pivot of a body, but the grinding reorientation of a landscape. The air in the cavern thickened, becoming the consistency of cold oil. The ceaseless *thump-thump-thump* of the looms did not falter, but their rhythm changed, syncing into a single, dreadful heartbeat that vibrated up through the stone into the marrow of my teeth. The light from the threads flared, casting long, predatory shadows that licked at the walls like black flames.

He was not a man. He was a silhouette cut from the memory of one, filled with the substance of the place. His face was a study in erosion, features worn smooth by the constant flow of stolen time, yet sharp with an undimmed, fanatical focus. His eyes were not eyes, but pools of that same molten gold that ran through the warp of the great loom. He wore the ghost of a hacendero’s finery—a frock coat bleached of color, trousers swallowed by the stone at his feet. In his hands, he did not hold a shuttle, but a length of that pulsing crimson weft, which dripped from his fingers like liquid heart’s-blood.

His gaze passed over León, a flicker of minor recognition, a dismissed variable. Then it settled on me.

It was not a look. It was an assessment. A pressure. I felt it on my skin, a dry, parsing touch, as if he were reading the grain of my life, measuring its length, its tensile strength. Seeing if I was fit for the pattern.

“An irregularity,” he said. His voice was the sound of the looms given words, a deep, rhythmic rasp. It did not echo. It was absorbed by the hungering stone. “A loose thread. You have your mother’s stillness. And her unfortunate curiosity.”

I could not speak. The hook in my heart was being pulled, not by grief, but by that gaze. It sought to unravel me, to find the end of my thread and feed it into the nearest empty shuttle. My father’s thread glowed steadily beside him, a docile, brilliant filament. A part of the beautiful machine. The love I had carried, a searching, aching thing, met the love that had built this ossuary, and mine felt like a faint, foolish whisper against a deafening hymn.

León stepped in front of me, breaking the line of that gaze. His body was a tense wire. “It’s over, Álvaro. The valley is waking up.”

“The valley is remembering,” the Weaver corrected, his tone one of pedantic patience. “It is achieving its perfected state. No decay. No loss. Only the enduring pattern.” He lifted the crimson thread. “This is the artery. The love for this land. My love. It sustains the whole. All other threads… are tributaries.”

“You killed them,” I said, the words scraping out of a throat gone to dust. “You killed them to feed it.”

“I saved them from the river,” he said, and for the first time, a flicker of emotion crossed the eroded face—a zealous light. “The river of time that washes everything away. Shoes. People. Memories. Here, nothing is washed away. Here, everything is *kept*. Your father understands. See how he contributes. See the beauty he helps to weave.”

He gestured, and the central loom shivered. In its growing tapestry, I saw not images, but sensations: the profound silence of a fossil, the cool perfection of a museum piece, the sweet, static sadness of a pressed flower. It was the beauty of the dead, arranged forever. My father’s thread hummed with a scholar’s satisfaction. He was solving the ultimate equation: how to make a moment last for eternity. The cost, the screaming tributaries, were just data.

This was the obstacle. Not a monster to be fought, but a logic to be dismantled. A love that had declared war on mortality itself, and had drafted the dead as its eternal army. How do you fight a memory that has become a mountain? How do you argue with a love that has built a heaven out of a tomb?

León’s hand went to the machete at his belt. It was a gesture of profound futility, like raising a twig against a tide. The Weaver’s molten eyes noted it.

“You brought a knife,” he said, “into the weaving room.”

He did not move. But the cavern moved for him.

The stone walkway beneath our feet rippled like the skin of a great beast. From the shadows between the looms, figures detached themselves. They were not the half-formed, moaning things from the jungle. These were older, drier, woven more completely into the pattern. Men and women in the faded garb of a century past, their faces blank canvases of stone-dust, their eyes vacant sockets where a faint, captured light glimmered. They held tools—weaving battens, shears, spindles—all made of the same black iron. They moved with a dreadful, synchronized grace, their feet not quite touching the ground.

They were the perfected servants. The preserved.

León shoved me back toward the archway. “Run!”

But the archway was gone. In its place was a wall of seamless, threaded stone. We were in the belly of the machine.

The preserved ones closed in, a silent, spreading stain. León swung his machete. It bit into the shoulder of a woman in a frayed *rebozo*. There was no blood. Only a puff of gray dust and a sound like grinding teeth. The wound sealed itself in seconds, threads of stone knitting back together. She brought her iron batten down. León parried, the clash ringing a sharp, lonely note against the loom’s thunder. He was strong, faster than any man had a right to be, but he was one. And they were many.

I stumbled back, my heel catching on a loose stone. I fell against the frame of a smaller loom. The vibration of it shot through my bones. A thread of silver light—a recent tributary, still vibrating with panic—brushed my cheek. It was warm. Alive. I jerked away.

I saw León take a blow to the ribs from a spindle. He grunted, his breath leaving him in a cloud. Another batten caught his leg. He went down on one knee. The preserved circled him, a slow, grinding dance. They were not trying to kill him. They were trying to unmake him, to still his furious, irregular motion and prepare him for the shuttle.

The Weaver watched, his hands busy again with the crimson thread. I was beneath his notice now, a problem being efficiently solved.

This was the failure. Not a battle lost, but a understanding achieved. We were not equal to this. We were life, and this was a place that had made a covenant against life. León’s struggle was the last frantic beat of a heart in a world that had chosen to be a heart. My search was the last flicker of a match in a vault that worshipped perpetual darkness.

León looked up from the stone, his eyes finding mine across the chaos. They held no fear. Only a deep, weary apology. He had brought me to the source, as I had asked. And the source was a god that ate questions and gave back only silence.

A preserved man with a face like cracked clay reached for him. León did not raise the machete. His strength, that profound, earthy resilience, left him. He accepted it. The fight bled out of him, replaced by the same exhausted recognition that was freezing my own blood. We had seen the engine of the world, and it was invincible.

The Weaver nodded, a small, satisfied motion. “Integrate the irregularity,” he intoned.

The preserved hands closed on León. They did not drag him, but began to *guide* him, with a terrible, gentle firmness, toward an empty shuttle on a nearby loom. His thread would be bright. It would be strong. It would add a fierce, wild beauty to the pattern for centuries to come.

And I saw it happen—the symbolic death. Not his, not yet. But mine. The death of the hope that had carried me from the veranda of La Ceniza. The hope that I could find my father and bring him home. The hope that the story had a different ending. That love was stronger than loss.

It was not.
Here, love *was* loss, perfected and made permanent. My love for my father was a tiny, flawed echo of the Weaver’s monstrous, preserving love. My love had sought, his love possessed. Mine was a river. His was a glacier.

The hook in my heart tore free, and what poured out was not grief, but a vast, hollow silence. I had reached the end of my thread. I watched, empty, as the preserved ones positioned León before the shuttle. He did not look at me again. He was already gone, his spirit retreating inward, preparing for the unending weave. The machete clattered from his hand, a final, metallic sigh against the stone.

I was alone. Truly alone, for the first time since my mother’s shoes had slid into the brown god. The warmth of the ghost-teacup was a memory from another universe. The air was not the temperature of blood, but of the grave—cold, still, and final.

I sank down against the loom, the vibrations a funeral dirge. My hand fell into a shallow groove in the stone floor, worn by centuries of passing feet. I traced its contour. A path. Worn by life. Now still.

In the hollow silence, a new sound arose. Not from the cavern, but from the memory of it. The sound of the river. Not the slow, brown god, but its voice—a million whispering, rushing, chaotic voices. The sound of things moving, changing, *ending*. The sound the Weaver had built his eternity to silence.
